    "text": "Secure Water Treatment (SWaT) - iTrust (https://www.sutd.edu.sg/itrust/itrust-labs/datasets/dataset-characteristics/swat/)\nciteturn16search0 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: yesterday; Collected network traffic & all the values obtained from all the 51 sensors and actuators ... The Jupyter notebook available at GitHub repository by ngoclesydney presents a comprehensive implementation of deep learning-based anomaly detection techniques applied to the iTrust Secure Water Treatment (SWaT) dataset.\n\n### Secure Water Treatment (SWaT)\n\n##### Characteristics of dataset (SWaT.A1_Dec 2015)\n\n  1. 11 days of continuous operation: 7 under normal operation and 4 days with attack scenarios\n  2. Collected network traffic & all the values obtained from all the 51 sensors and actuators\n  3. Data labelled according to normal and abnormal behaviours\n  4. Attack Scenarios: Derived through the attack models developed by our research team. The attack model considers the intent space of a CPS as an attack model. 41 attacks were launched during the 4 days and are described in the PDF.\n\nThe Jupyter notebook available at GitHub repository by ngoclesydney presents a comprehensive implementation of deep learning-based anomaly detection techniques applied to the iTrust Secure Water Treatment (SWaT) dataset. This repository develops novel security metrics using advanced deep learning approaches to detect anomalous attacks in critical infrastructure systems. The implementation leverages 1D (time) Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN) and Recurrent Neural Networks (RNN) specifically designed to identify anomalous attacks in Industrial Control Systems (ICS). The SWaT dataset, represents a scaled-down but high-fidelity replica of a modern six-stage water treatment facility that processes approximately 19 litres of water per minute. This dataset is particularly valuable for cybersecurity research as it contains both normal operational data and records of various cyber-physical attacks. This repo implements deep learning models using Google’s TensorFlow framework version 2.x and the Keras library.\n\n#### Updates on dataset\n\n \n\n24 Sep 18 (SWaT.A2_Dec 2015)\nTwo sets of “SWaT_Dataset_Normal” – versions 0 and 1 – are provided. The datasets capture the normal state of the SWaT testbed running for seven days. In Version 0, we started recording the data when the plant was emptying the water storage tank for 30 minutes. In general, in an ICS environment, this is part of the maintenance outside normal operations. As a result of this drainage, the first 30 minutes of LIT101 data exhibits change even though there was no water in/outflow. Version 1 is derived from version 0 by removing the first 30 minutes of data.\n\n \n\nSWaT.A3_Jun 2017\n136 hours of network traffic and historian data from continuously running SWaT (no attacks) was collected over 6 days.\n\n \n\n14 Aug 19 (SWaT.A4_Jul 2019)\nA new set of SWaT dataset, collected during Jul 2019, is available for downloading. This set includes 3 hours of SWaT running under normal operating condition and 1 hour during which 6 attacks were carried out. Those who have previously received SWaT dataset download link can use the same link to access this new dataset.\n\n \n\n23 Oct 19 (SWaT.A5_Jul 2019)\nWe received queries on the SWaT dataset, collected during Jul 2019, e.g., under LS 201 the fields were recorded as “{u’IsSystem’: False, u’Name’: u’Inactive’, u’Value’: 0}”. These fields have been updated to “Active” or “Inactive” and the dataset saved as version 2. The fields’ definitions are provided in the “readme.docx” document that was shared along with the dataset. Those who were given the link to download the dataset previously can download the new files using the same link.\n\n \n\n19 Dec 19 (SWaT.A6_Dec 2019)\nA new set of SWaT dataset, collected during Dec 2019, is available for downloading. The dataset consists of pcap and Historian Data (.csv) files. The dataset records a series of malware infection attacks on the SWaT Engineering Workstation. The malware attacks include Historian Data Exfiltration attack and Process Disruption attacks.\n\nThis set includes 3 hours of SWaT running under normal operating condition and 1 hour in which 6 attacks were carried out. Those who have previously received SWaT dataset download link can use the same link to access this new dataset.\n\n \n\n9 Jul 2020 (SWaT.A7_June 2020)\nSWaT was run on 4 occasions (no attack). Each run lasted either 2 or 4 hours. Network traffic data was captured for the 4 runs.\n\n \n\nSWaT Dec 2023 (100-hour run)\nCharacteristics of the Datasets 18 Dec – 22 Dec 2023 SWaT.A9_18 Dec 2023 & WaDi.A3_18 Dec 2023 datasets, i.e., 100 Hour Run Dataset\n\n  1. 105 hours of continuous operations of both SWaT and WaDi testbeds under normal conditions.\n  2. The first 5 hours of continuous run of both testbeds was not in CrossOver mode, i.e., each testbed was run in a closed loop.\n  3. The testbeds were run for the remaining 100 hours in CrossOver mode.\n  4. No attacks were performed, i.e., Clean Dataset.\n  5. The flow rate of the WaDi Consumer Tank 2 was deliberately set to 0.00 m^3/h to avoid triggering its faulty sensor.\n  6. The following files are available:\n    1. The PCAPs for the 100 Hour Run Dataset\n      1. Total number of files and respective data size: 4,106 x 190MB files totalling 764 GB\n      2. WaDi: 1,804 x 190MB files totalling 335 GB\n      3. SWaT: 2,302 x 190MB files totalling 429 GB\n    2. The Historian Datasets for both SWaT and WaDi\n    3. The NetFlow logs (Incomplete Set – The Net_Flow VM was not able to capture the full run due to its unavailability)--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nWater Distribution (WaDi) - iTrust (https://www.sutd.edu.sg/itrust/itrust-labs/datasets/dataset-characteristics/wadi/)\nciteturn16search1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: yesterday; Data from all the 123 sensors and actuators ... Data from all the 123 sensors and actuators\n  3. Attack Scenarios: Derived from the attack models developed by our research team. The attack model considers the intent space of a CPS as an attack model. 15 attacks were launched during the 2 days.\n\n \n\n \n\n \n\n#### Updates on dataset\n\n \n\n19 Dec 19 (WaDi.A2)\nAs the plant was unstable for certain periods during the operation, the affected readings have been removed and a new csv file “WADI_14days_new.csv” uploaded. A second csv file “WADI_attackdataLABLE.csv” now contains labels on whether there was an attack (-1) or not (1). The updated attack table with the corrected dates has also been uploaded.\n\n \n\nWaDi Dec 2023 (100-hour run)\nCharacteristics of the Datasets 18 Dec – 22 Dec 2023 SWaT.A9_18 Dec 2023 & WaDi.A3_18 Dec 2023 datasets, i.e., 100 Hour Run Dataset\n\n  1. 105 hours of continuous operations of both SWaT and WaDi testbeds under normal conditions.\n  2. The first 5 hours of continuous run of both testbeds was not in CrossOver mode, i.e., each testbed was run in a closed loop.\n  3. The testbeds were run for the remaining 100 hours in CrossOver mode.\n  4. No attacks were performed, i.e., Clean Dataset.\n  5. The flow rate of the WaDi Consumer Tank 2 was deliberately set to 0.00 m^3/h to avoid triggering its faulty sensor.\n  6. The following files are available:\n    1. The PCAPs for the 100 Hour Run Dataset\n      1. Total number of files and respective data size: 4,106 x 190MB files totalling 764 GB\n      2. WaDi: 1,804 x 190MB files totalling 335 GB\n      3. SWaT: 2,302 x 190MB files totalling 429 GB\n    2. The Historian Datasets for both SWaT and WaDi\n    3. Nguyen · Updated 3 years ago\n\narrow_drop_up[Button: 16]\n\ncode Code\n\nfile_download Download\n\nImage\n\n[Button: more_vert]\n\n# BATADAL: Cyber Attacks Detection in Water Systems\n\nCyber Attacks Detection in Water Distribution Systems Using Deep Learning.\n\nImage\n\n## BATADAL: Cyber Attacks Detection in Water Systems\n\nData CardCode (4)Discussion (1)Suggestions (0)\n\n## About Dataset\n\n### Insights On Cyberbiosecurity Field\n\nAccording to Cyberbiosecurity: A New Perspective on Protecting U.S. Food and Agricultural System: \"The US's national data and infrastructure security issues affecting the “bioeconomy” are evolving rapidly. Simultaneously, the conversation about cyber security of the U.S. food and agricultural system (cyber biosecurity) is incomplete and disjointed. The food and agricultural production sectors influence over 20% of the nation's economy ($6.7T) and 15% of U.S. employment (43.3M jobs). The food and agricultural sectors are immensely diverse and they require advanced technologies and efficiencies that rely on computer technologies, big data, cloud-based data storage, and internet accessibility. There is a critical need to safeguard the cyber biosecurity of our bio economy, but currently protections are minimal and do not broadly exist across the food and agricultural system.\"\n\nCyberbiosecurity is an emerging discipline for protecting life sciences data, functions, operation, and the bio-economy.\nImage\n\n### Insights On The Dataset\n\nThe BATtle of the Attack Detection ALgorithms (BATADAL) will objectively compare the performance of algorithms for the detection of cyber attacks in water distribution systems.\n\nC-Town Public Utility (CPU) is the main water distribution system operator of C-Town (Figure 1). For many years, CPU has operated a static distribution topology. In the last year, CPU has introduced novel smart technology to enable remote data collection from sensors in the field, and remote control of actuators.   * C-Town consists of 388 nodes linked with 429 pipes and is divided into 5 district metered areas (DMAs).\n  * More specifically, the SCADA data include the water level at all 7 tanks of the network (T1–T7), status and flow of all 11 pumps (PU1–PU11) and the one actuated valve (V2) of the network, and pressure at 24 pipes of the network that correspond to the inlet and outlet pressure of the pumps and the actuated valve.\n\nImage\n\nGraph Annotation:\n\n  * L_T #: water level of a tank # [meter].​\n  * S_PU # or S_V # : status of a pump # or a valve # [dmnl]. Binary signal.​\n  * F_PU # or F_V # : flowrate of a pump # or a valve # [L/s].​\n  * P_J # : inlet and outlet pressure for a junction # [meter].\n\nDataset Details (TL:DR):\n\n  * There are 43 columns and a 1/0 label column, with 1 meaning that the system is under attack and 0 meaning that the system is in normal operation.\n  * Training Dataset 1: This dataset was released on November 20 2016, and it was generated from a one-year long simulation. The dataset does not contain any attacks, i.e. all the data pertains to C-Town normal operations.\n  * Training Dataset 2: This dataset with partially labeled data was released on November 28 2016. The dataset is around 6 months long and contains several attacks, some of which are approximately labeled.\n  * Test Dataset: This 3-months long dataset contains several attacks but no labels. The dataset was released on February 20 2017, and it is used to compare the performance of the algorithms (see rules document for details).\n\n### Notes\n\n  * More information about the BATADAL dataset and the 2017-2018 competition can be found in here. Paper of the competition is publicly available here.\n  * The dataset is created and published by the BATADAL team. The dataset sharing followed CC BY 4.0. If there are any additional changes or questions regarding the dataset, please don't hesitate to contact me.\n  * This is a very good dataset (I would consider it as one of the two benchmark datasets, besides SWaT dataset) in the applied AI in water distribution research. Many research papers have been published, but only a handful published their codes and analysis. I hope to bring Kagglers' awareness and attention to this field, experience, and contribute. After all, water is awesome!\n\nReferences:\n\n  * Duncan, Susan E., et al. \"Cyberbiosecurity: A new perspective on protecting US food and agricultural system.\" Frontiers in bioengineering and biotechnology 7 (2019): 63.\n  * Taormina, Riccardo, et al. \"Battle of the attack detection algorithms: Disclosing cyber attacks on water distribution networks.\" Journal of Water Resources Planning and Management 144.8 (2018): 04018048.\n\n\n## License\n\nAttribution 4.0 International (CC BY 4.0)\n\n## Tags\n\nBusinessEarth and NatureDeep LearningTime Series AnalysisWater TransportOutlier Analysis\n\n## Metadata\n\ninsights\n\n## Activity Overview\n\n### Detail View\n\n[Button: keyboard_arrow_up]--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nBATADAL Dataset (https://www.kaggle.com/code/favadhassanjaskani/batadal-dataset/input)\nciteturn16search4 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 4 days ago; EPIC</td></tr>\n<tr><td>121</td><td>Junjie He</td><td>South China University of Technology</td><td>China</td><td>10-Jul-18</td><td>SWaT &amp; EPIC</td></tr>\n</table>--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nNoisePrint was designed using data from the SWaT historian. For the earlier published results of NoisePrint [AsiaCCS-2018] data was taken from the SWaT historian at a sampling rate of one sample per second. During the CISS event NoisePrint received the samples from Clone Historian¹ where sample frequency was between 1-3 seconds depending on the number of requests to Clone Historian. The noise pattern being sensitive to sampling rate led to unexpected performance. Secondly, the recent data used to create models was collected for the states when the plant was operational, the author realised during the CISS event when a lot of false alarms were observed when plant was not active. In future, the author can obtain the data from the plant for all states (active and idle) using the Clone Historian but not the historian. (https://itrust.sutd.edu.sg/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/CISS-Technical-Report-2019_final_website.pdf)\nciteturn16search13 [wordlim: 200] Published: 3.5 years ago; NoisePrint was designed using data from the SWaT historian. ... ### 6.2.5 Combined iTrust ADMs ... <td>51</td>\n<td>-</td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td>b) HMI/SCADA values</td>\n<td>7</td>\n<td>-</td>\n<td>6</td>\n<td>-</td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td>c) PLC values</td>\n<td>24</td>\n<td>-</td>\n<td>11</td>\n<td>-</td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td>d) Remote I/O values</td>\n<td>3</td>\n<td>-</td>\n<td>3</td>\n<td>-</td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td>IT Anomalies</td>\n<td>54</td>\n<td>-</td>\n<td>38</td>\n<td>-</td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td>OT Anomalies</td>\n<td>90</td>\n<td>29%</td>\n<td>46</td>\n<td>41%</td>\n</tr>\n<tr>\n<td>Total based on technology</td>\n<td>51</td>\n<td>43%</td>\n<td>23</td>\n<td>83%</td>\n</tr>\n</table>\n\n## 7 Conclusion\n\nSeven Red Teams and five Blue Teams (4 commercial products and an iTrust Blue Team featuring six ADMs). The Red Teams were awarded points based on the type of attacks launched, point of entry, physical process and sensor data goals and the level of precision the attacker had during the execution. The Blue Teams were evaluated based on the number of anomalies detected arising from successful and unsuccessful attacks. The Blue Teams were scored using the submitted evidence (of attack detection), based on the attack logs provided\n\n¹ Clone Historian acts as a Client for the primary Historian and a Server for Blue Teams and allows multiple requests to retrieve data from the SWaT Historian, thereby reducing the sampling load on the Historian\n\nPage 16 | 17--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nSWat Project (https://www.kaggle.com/code/nadaemad2002/swat-project)\nciteturn16search5 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 2 weeks ago; Summary of the Problem: I recently public the dataset \"BATADAL: Cyber Attacks Detection in Water Systems\". I was able to access the dataset via URL in incognito mode and was able to see it on my dashboard when I log in. However, the dataset is not searchable in the search bar or not available on the \"Public\" section on my dashboard in incognito mode.\nImage\n\n  2. Steps to reproduce: You can try to access it via here. But you won't be able to search for it or find it on my dashboard.\n\n  3. Expected behavior: My guess is that Kaggle does periodic updates on its database/search engine and I missed the update deadline when I made it from private to public. It would be nice to know if this is a reason, if so, I wouldn't mind waiting for the system to update.\n\nComments and feedbacks are welcome~\n\nThanks!\n\n\n## 4 Comments\n\nHotness\n\n[Input]\n\nZach Diemer\n\nPosted 4 years ago\n\narrow_drop_up[Button: 4]\n\n[Button: more_vert]\n\nTaking a look for you. Finally, we determined that this algorithm was able to estimate the water’s turbidity acceptably and supports the operators in making decisions for the application of adequate treatment to drinking water.\n\n3 April 2023\n\n# Estimation of Water Turbidity in Drinking Water Treatment Plants Using Machine Learning Based on Water and Meteorological Data^{ †}\n\nVanessa Fernandez Alvarez^{*},\n\nDaniela Granada Salazar,\n\nCristhian Figueroa,\n\nJuan Carlos Corrales and\n\nJuan Fernando Casanova\n\nDepartment of Telematics, University of Cauca, Popayan 190002, Colombia\n\n^{*}\n\nAuthor to whom correspondence should be addressed.\n\n^{†}\n\nPresented at the 7th International Electronic Conference on Water Sciences, 15–30 March 2023; Available online: https://ecws-7.sciforum.net.\n\nEnviron. Sci. Proc.2023, 25(1), 89;https://doi.org/10.3390/ECWS-7-14326\n\nThis article belongs to the Proceedings The 7th International Electronic Conference on Water Sciences\n\nVersion Notes\n\nOrder Reprints\n\n## Abstract\n\nIn rural areas, water treatment plants use rudimentary techniques to evaluate turbidity. However, the incorrect measurement of turbidity can result in poor water quality and, as a result, health issues for its users because it is a crucial indicator to determine the application of adequate treatment to the water. Aquarisc was a project financed with royalties that sought to strengthen the mechanisms and tools for decision-making of the authorities and territorial institutions related to water supply for human consumption. This project installed sensors to assess turbidity in some plants in rural areas of the department of Cauca, Colombia. However, when the project ended, these sensors were removed. Therefore, it became necessary to create machine learning models to predict turbidity values without sensors, considering only pH, temperature, vapor pressure, and precipitation data captured manually by plant operators. In this study, the Linear Regression, Random Forest Regressor, k-Neighbors Regressor, and Extra Trees Regressor algorithms were trained with data provided by the Aquarisc project and the Institute of Hydrology, Meteorology, and Environment Studies of Colombia (IDEAM). As a result, we selected the Random Forest Regressor since it had the best RMSE among all the models and was also the one that best matched the situation of the studied treatment plants. Furthermore, this model did not consider outliers, resulting in an RMSE of 20.98 and 3.49 for the training and test dataset, respectively. Finally, we determined that this algorithm was able to estimate the water’s turbidity acceptably and supports the operators in making decisions for the application of adequate treatment to drinking water.\n\nKeywords:\n\nturbidity; water treatment; random forest; turbidity estimation\n\n## 1. Introduction\n\nThe quality of water supplied by potable water treatment plants is crucial for ensuring consumers’ health. Turbidity is one of the most critical parameters to determine this quality, as it indicates the presence of colloidal, mineral, and organic substances in the water [1].\n\nThe presence of turbidity in water stimulates the proliferation of bacteria and provides coverage and food for pathogens in the water. Moreover, it hinders proper water disinfection and risks consumer health [2]. For this reason, entities supplying water for human consumption in Colombia must ensure compliance with the water quality standards established in Resolution 2115 of 2007, which establishes that turbidity should not exceed 2 Nephelometric Turbidity Units (NTU) [3].\n\nCurrently, there are various pieces of equipment and instruments, such as turbidity tubes, Secchi disks, and spectrophotometers, which are used to measure the turbidity of water. However, according to studies carried out by [4,5], and the project “Vulnerability and Risk in Drinking Water Systems of Cauca–Aquarisc”, it has been demonstrated that some municipal water treatment plants in Colombia face difficulties in determining the physicochemical conditions of the water. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nEstimation of Water Turbidity in Drinking Water Treatment Plants Using Machine Learning Based on Water and Meteorological Data \"2279 (https://pdfs.semanticscholar.org/4d0a/1a44449d97ba8eacc100a2de0ee1f119207d.pdf)\nciteturn18search12 [wordlim: 200] Published: 3.4 years ago; Estimation of Water Turbidity in Drinking Water Treatment ... [T/10,10,N]\\r\\n    yg = y2.reshape(-1, DOWNSAMPLE_SEC)               # [T/10,10]\\r\\n\\r\\n    # paper-style: median for features\\r\\n    X2 = np.median(Xg, axis=1)\\r\\n\\r\\n    # label: majority vote\\r\\n    y2 = (yg.mean(axis=1) >= 0.5).astype(np.int64)\\r\\n\\r\\nprint(\\\"final X2:\\\", X2.shape, \\\"final y2:\\\", y2.shape, \\\"counts:\\\", {0:int((y2==0).sum()), 1:int((y2==1).sum())})\\r\\n\\r\\nCELL 4 normal_data = X2[y2 == 0]\\r\\nattack_data = X2[y2 == 1]\\r\\n\\r\\nprint(\\\"normal_data:\\\", normal_data.shape)\\r\\nprint(\\\"attack_data:\\\", attack_data.shape)\\r\\n\\r\\nCELL 5 mn = normal_data.min(axis=0)\\r\\nmx = normal_data.max(axis=0)\\r\\ndenom = np.where((mx - mn) < 1e-12, 1.0, (mx - mn))\\r\\n\\r\\nnormal_n = np.clip((normal_data - mn) / denom, 0.0, 1.0)\\r\\nattack_n = np.clip((attack_data - mn) / denom, 0.0, 1.0)\\r\\n\\r\\nprint(\\\"normal_n range:\\\", float(normal_n.min()), float(normal_n.max()))\\r\\nprint(\\\"attack_n range:\\\", float(attack_n.min()), float(attack_n.max()))\\r\\n\\r\\nCELL 8 # ===== Cell A: Window dataset & loaders =====\\r\\n\\r\\nimport torch\\r\\nimport numpy as np\\r\\nfrom torch.utils.data import Dataset, DataLoader\\r\\nimport random\\r\\n\\r\\ndef set_seed(seed=42):\\r\\n    random.seed(seed)\\r\\n    np.random.seed(seed)\\r\\n    torch.manual_seed(seed)\\r\\n    torch.cuda.manual_seed_all(seed)\\r\\n\\r\\nset_seed(42)\\r\\n\\r\\ndevice = \\\"cuda\\\" if torch.cuda.is_available() else \\\"cpu\\\"\\r\\nprint(\\\"device:\\\", device)\\r\\n\\r\\nclass WindowDataset(Dataset):\\r\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\r\\n    X(t) = [s(t-w), ..., s(t-1)]  shape [w, N]\\r\\n    y(t) = s(t)                  shape [N]\\r\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\r\\n    def __init__(self, data, labels=None, w=16):\\r\\n        self.data = data.astype(np.float32)\\r\\n        self.labels = None if labels is None else labels.astype(np.int64)\\r\\n        self.w = int(w)\\r\\n\\r\\n    def __len__(self):\\r\\n        return len(self.data) - self.w\\r\\n\\r\\n    def __getitem__(self, idx):\\r\\n        x = self.data[idx: idx + self.w]      # [w, N]\\r\\n        y = self.data[idx + self.w]           # [N]\\r\\n        lab = 0 if self.labels is None else int(self.labels[idx + self.w])\\r\\n        return torch.from_numpy(x), torch.from_numpy(y), torch.tensor(lab, dtype=torch.long)\\r\\n\\r\\n# window & batch size (paper)\\r\\nW = 16\\r\\nB = 64\\r\\nN = train_n.shape[1]\\r\\n\\r\\ntrain_loader = DataLoader(\\r\\n    WindowDataset(train_n, labels=None, w=W),\\r\\n    batch_size=B, shuffle=True, drop_last=True\\r\\n)\\r\\n\\r\\nval_loader = DataLoader(\\r\\n    WindowDataset(val_n, labels=None, w=W),\\r\\n    batch_size=B, shuffle=False, drop_last=False\\r\\n)\\r\\n\\r\\nprint(\\\"train windows:\\\", len(train_loader.dataset))\\r\\nprint(\\\"val windows  :\\\", len(val_loader.dataset))\\r\\n\\r\\nCELL 9 # ===== Cell C: Paper-sized splits (SWaT) =====\\r\\n\\r\\nTRAIN_LEN = 49500\\r\\nTEST_LEN  = 44992\\r\\n\\r\\n# 1) Separate normal rows for fitting scaling (fit on ALL normal)\\r\\nnormal_rows = X2[y2 == 0]\\r\\nprint(\\\"Downsampled total:\\\", X2.shape, \\\"normal:\\\", normal_rows.shape, \\\"attack:\\\", int((y2==1).sum()))\\r\\n\\r\\n# Fit MinMax on NORMAL only\\r\\nmn = normal_rows.min(axis=0)\\r\\nmx = normal_rows.max(axis=0)\\r\\nden = np.where((mx - mn) < 1e-12, 1.0, (mx - mn))\\r\\n\\r\\nX2n = np.clip((X2 - mn) / den, 0.0, 1.0)  # ===== Cell E: WindowDataset & DataLoaders (paper-sized) =====\\r\\n\\r\\nimport torch\\r\\nimport numpy as np\\r\\nfrom torch.utils.data import Dataset, DataLoader\\r\\nimport random\\r\\n\\r\\ndef set_seed(seed=42):\\r\\n    random.seed(seed)\\r\\n    np.random.seed(seed)\\r\\n    torch.manual_seed(seed)\\r\\n    torch.cuda.manual_seed_all(seed)\\r\\n\\r\\nset_seed(42)\\r\\n\\r\\ndevice = \\\"cuda\\\" if torch.cuda.is_available() else \\\"cpu\\\"\\r\\nprint(\\\"device:\\\", device)\\r\\n\\r\\nclass WindowDataset(Dataset):\\r\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\r\\n    Paper definition:\\r\\n    X(t) = [s(t-16), ..., s(t-1)]  shape [16, N]\\r\\n    y(t) = s(t)                   shape [N]\\r\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\r\\n    def __init__(self, data, labels=None, w=16):\\r\\n        self.data = data.astype(np.float32)\\r\\n        self.labels = None if labels is None else labels.astype(np.int64)\\r\\n        self.w = int(w)\\r\\n\\r\\n    def __len__(self):\\r\\n        return len(self.data) - self.w\\r\\n\\r\\n    def __getitem__(self, idx):\\r\\n        x = self.data[idx: idx + self.w]   # [w, N]\\r\\n        y = self.data[idx + self.w]        # [N]\\r\\n        lab = 0 if self.labels is None else int(self.labels[idx + self.w])\\r\\n        return torch.from_numpy(x), torch.from_numpy(y), torch.tensor(lab, dtype=torch.long)\\r\\n\\r\\nW = 16\\r\\nB = 64\\r\\nN = train_n.shape[1]\\r\\n\\r\\ntrain_loader = DataLoader(\\r\\n    WindowDataset(train_n, labels=None, w=W),\\r\\n    batch_size=B, shuffle=True, drop_last=True\\r\\n)\\r\\n\\r\\ntest_loader = DataLoader(\\r\\n    WindowDataset(test_seq, labels=test_lab, w=W),\\r\\n    batch_size=B, shuffle=False, drop_last=False\\r\\n)\\r\\n\\r\\nprint(\\\"Train windows:\\\", len(train_loader.dataset))\\r\\nprint(\\\"Test windows :\\\", len(test_loader.dataset))\\r\\nprint(\\\"Sensors      :\\\", N)\\r\\n\\r\\nCELL 19 # ===== Cell L: Paper scoring (median+IQR graph deviation) =====\\r\\n\\r\\nimport numpy as np\\r\\n\\r\\ndef moving_average(a, win):\\r\\n    if win <= 1:\\r\\n        return a\\r\\n    pad = win - 1\\r\\n    ap = np.pad(a, (pad, 0), mode=\\\"edge\\\")\\r\\n    c = np.cumsum(ap, dtype=float)\\r\\n    out = (c[win:] - c[:-win]) / win\\r\\n    return out\\r\\n\\r\\ndef precision_recall_f1(y_true, y_pred):\\r\\n    y_true = y_true.astype(np.int64)\\r\\n    y_pred = y_pred.astype(np.int64)\\r\\n    tp = int(((y_true == 1) & (y_pred == 1)).sum())\\r\\n    fp = int(((y_true == 0) & (y_pred == 1)).sum())\\r\\n    fn = int(((y_true == 1) & (y_pred == 0)).sum())\\r\\n    pr = tp / (tp + fp + 1e-12)\\r\\n    re = tp / (tp + fn + 1e-12)\\r\\n    f1 = 2 * pr * re / (pr + re + 1e-12)\\r\\n    return pr, re, f1\\r\\n\\r\\n@torch.no_grad()\\r\\ndef collect_abs_forecast_errors(loader):\\r\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\r\\n    Returns:\\r\\n      err: [T_windows, N] absolute error per sensor\\r\\n      lab: [T_windows] window labels\\r\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\r\\n    model.eval()\\r\\n    errs = []\\r\\n    labs = []\\r\\n    for x, y, lab in loader:\\r\\n        x = x.to(device)\\r\\n        y = y.to(device)\\r\\n\\r\\n        yhat, _, _, _, _ = model(x, process_groups)\\r\\n        err = (yhat - y).abs().cpu().numpy()  # [B,N]\\r\\n\\r\\n        errs.append(err)\\r\\n        labs.append(lab.numpy())\\r\\n    return np.concatenate(errs, axis=0), np.concatenate(labs, axis=0)\\r\\n\\r\\n# 1) Validation errors (normal) -> median + IQR per sensor\\r\\nval_err, _ = collect_abs_forecast_errors(val_loader)   # University of Cauca\nL71: \nL72: Abstract\nL73: The measurement of raw water turbidity is essential to determine its degree of potabilization. However, an erroneous measurement of this treatment can cause the water to be of insufficient quality to be purified, generating health problems for consumers.\nL74: Unfortunately, in rural treatment plants in Colombia, it is common to find this type of deficiency because they use rudimentary techniques to evaluate turbidity, making it impossible to guarantee sufficient quality for water to be considered fit for human consumption.\nL75: This article proposes the use of machine learning models in the treatment plants from the municipalities of Santander de Quilichao, Timbío, and Mercaderes in the department of Cauca (Colombia), in order to estimate the turbidity value of raw water considering only available data such as pH, temperature, vapor pressure, and precipitation captured manually by plant's operators.\nL76: To develop these modeles, Linear Regression, Random Forest Regressor, Kneighbors Regressor, and Extra Trees Regressor algorithms were trained with data provided by the Aquarisc project and the Colombian Institute of Hydrology, Meteorology and Environment Studies (IDEAM). The study determined that the best-performing algorithm in this context was the Random Forest Regressor.\nL77: This algorithm had the best RMSE among all the models and was also the one that best fitted the situation of the treatment plants studied. Furthermore, this model did not consider outliers and obtained a result of an RMSE of 20.98 and 3.49 for the training and test data sets, respectively. Finally, it was determined that this algorithm could acceptably estimate the water's turbidity and may support the operators in the decision-making process for applying an adequate treatment to the drinking water.\nL78: Keywords\nL79: \nL80: Turbidity\nL81: \nL82: water treatment\nL83: \nL84: random forest\nL85: \nL86: water treatment plants\nL87: \nL88: turbidity estimation\nL89: \nL90: Manuscript\nL91: \nL92: sciforum-068848.pdf\nL93: \nL94: Download  Preview\nL95: \nL96: Water Security and Environmental Impact Assessment: A Study for Developing Economies\nL97: \nL98: Previous\nL99: \nL100: The generalized translog specification cost function to estimate drinking water Tarif: Case of Tunisia\nL101: \nL102: Next\nL103: ###  Further Information\nL104: \nL105:   * cite1†Find events L106:   * cite2†Pricing L107: \nL108: ###  Resources\nL109: \nL110:   * cite4†Contact L111:   * cite3†FAQs L112: \nL113: ###  Follow Sciforum\nL114: \nL115:   * cite10†Twitter†twitter.com L116:   * cite11†LinkedIn†www.linkedin.com L117:   * cite12†Facebook†www.facebook.com L118: ###  MDPI Initiatives\nL119: \nL120:   * cite13†MDPI Books†www.mdpi.com L121:   * cite14†Preprints†www.preprints.org L122:   * cite15†Scilit†www.scilit.com L123:   * cite16†SciProfiles†sciprofiles.com L124: \nL125:   * cite17†Encyclopedia†encyclopedia.pub L126:   * cite18†JAMS†jams.pub L127:   * cite19†Proceedings Series†www.mdpi.com L128:   * cite20†Author Services†www.mdpi.com L129: \nL130: * * *\nL131: \nL132: © 2026 cite21† MDPI †www.mdpi.com (Basel, Switzerland) unless otherwise stated\nL133: \nL134: Disclaimer\nL135: \nL136: Disclaimer\nL137: \nL138: cite22†Terms and Conditions cite23†Privacy Policy Privacy Settings cite24†Accessibility†www.mdpi.com "

Link a period rollforward | Link this month's Beginning inventory to the prior month's Ending inventory; calculate Ending as Beginning + Receipts − Usage. | Rebuild cumulative history from the first month in every period when the prior ending balance already represents the same quantity. |\\r\\n| F5. Preserve rule boundaries | For supplied bands `0 ≤ x < 100`, `100 ≤ x < 500`, and `x ≥ 500`, preserve those boundaries and test the thresholds and values on either side. | Turn `<100` into `≤100`, reorder overlapping tests, fill an intentional gap or invent a default category. |\\r\\n| F8. Guard the expected exception | With validated numeric B8 and C8 and a not-applicable policy for a zero denominator, use `=IF(C8=0,\\\"n.a.\\\",B8/C8)`. A deliberate blank may be appropriate under a different display policy. | Use `IFERROR(...,0)` so missing data or a broken reference appears to be a real zero rate. |\\r\\n| F9. User instructions and preferences, reference/template conventions and domain guidance take precedence, in that order. For edits, do not change unrelated content outside of the user's request and follow the workbook’s existing writing style.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Write for the intended audience. Bad: `Follow the weekly trends`\\r\\n  - Use `Metric` for a general metric column and `Revenue driver` for a revenue assumption explanation. Good: `Hiring requests awaiting approval` when approval is the issue.\\r\\n  - Bad: `Two-band rating movement`. Bad: `Decision-ready monthly impact analysis`\\r\\n  - Good: `Income and household assumptions`. Bad: `Same paycheck. Different purchasing power.`\\r\\n  - Use `Retained employees` only for employees who remained over a defined period. Otherwise, name the population counted, such as `Total employees` or `Employees reviewed`.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Avoid decorative bullets, icons, emoji, arrows and pipe-delimited titles. Omit filler suffixes; keep terms such as `review`, `analysis` or `dashboard` when they identify the content.\\r\\n  - Bad: `$ in USD • monthly • forecast`\\r\\n  - Good: `Monthly forecast (USD)`\\r\\n\\r\\n- Prefer direct, specific human wording. Trace output can be large, so summarize by depth/node count before logging.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Inspect for workbook understanding\\r\\n- Compact summary:\\r\\n```js\\r\\nawait wb.inspect({\\r\\n  kind: \\\"workbook,sheet,table\\\",\\r\\n  maxChars: 6000,\\r\\n  tableMaxRows: 6,\\r\\n  tableMaxCols: 6,\\r\\n  tableMaxCellChars: 80,\\r\\n});\\r\\n```\\r\\n- Quick overview of sheet ids and names: `await wb.inspect({ kind: \\\"sheet\\\", include: \\\"id,name\\\" })`\\r\\n- Formula discovery in a targeted area: `await wb.inspect({ kind: \\\"formula\\\", sheetId: firstSheetName, range: \\\"A1:Z30\\\", maxChars: 2500, options: {maxResults:50} })`\\r\\n- Checking existing styles in a targeted area: `await wb.inspect({ kind: \\\"computedStyle\\\", sheetId: firstSheetName, range: \\\"A1:E10\\\", maxChars: 2500 })`\\r\\n- Common `kind` tokens: `workbook`, `sheet`, `table`, `region`, `match`, `formula`, `thread`, `computedStyle`, `definedName`, `drawing`\\r\\n- Inspects can also be used to zoom in on specific areas, especially for target edits:\\r\\n```js\\r\\nawait wb.inspect({\\r\\n  kind: \\\"region\\\",\\r\\n  sheetId: firstSheetName,\\r\\n  range: \\\"A1:Z30\\\",\\r\\n  maxChars: 2500,\\r\\n});\\r\\n```\\r\\n- Inspect output may include JSON records with `\\\"id\\\"` values (for example `\\\"ws/r5qsk5\\\"`), which you can resolve back to workbook objects with `wb.resolve(...)`:\\r\\n- `wb.resolve(\\\"ws/...\\\")` -> worksheet\\r\\n- `wb.resolve(\\\"th/...\\\")` -> comment thread\\r\\n\\r\\n## Known Gotchas (Do not repeat)\\r\\n- Do not set undocumented attributes on remote objects.\\r\\n- `Workbook.create()` starts with no sheets; add one before calling `getActiveWorksheet()`.\\r\\n- Create every worksheet referenced by formulas before writing cross-sheet formulas.\\r\\n- Prefer bounded formula ranges, e.g. `$A$6:$A$205`. Full-column references can be truncated to each column's populated extent: unequal extents can make `COUNTIFS` / `SUMIFS` return `#VALUE!`, and `ROWS` / `INDEX` can differ from Excel.\\r\\n- If export fails, isolate the cause by checkpoint-export after major blocks to isolate the cause: base sheets, values/formulas, formatting, conditional formatting, tables, charts/rendering. For charts, first simplify optional styling first: nested border configs, custom chart axis/series mutations, broad autofit/formatting, then nonessential drawings.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Quick API Surface (High-Value + Common)\\r\\n\\r\\n### Core workbook/file APIs\\r\\n- `import { FileBlob, SpreadsheetFile, Workbook } from \\\"@oai/artifact-tool\\\"`\\r\\n- `const workbook = Workbook.create()` to create an empty workbook\\r\\n- `const sheet = workbook.worksheets.add(\\\"Sheet1\\\")` to add a sheet\\r\\n- `const workbook = await SpreadsheetFile.importXlsx(arrayBufferOrFileBlob)`\\r\\n- `const xlsx = await SpreadsheetFile.exportXlsx(workbook); await xlsx.save(\\\"output.xlsx\\\")`\\r\\n- `const inspect = await workbook.inspect({ kind: \\\"sheet\\\", include: \\\"id,name\\\", sheetId, range: \\\"A1:C10\\\" })`\\r\\n- `const help = workbook.help(\\\"worksheet.getRange\\\", { include: \\\"index,examples\\\" })`\\r\\n- Preferred: `const preview = await workbook.render({ sheetName: \\\"Sheet1\\\", autoCrop: \\\"all\\\", scale: 1, format: \\\"png\\\" })`\\r\\n- To get the bytes and/or save the blob to file:\\r\\n```js\\r\\nconst previewBytes = new Uint8Array(await preview.arrayBuffer());\\r\\nawait fs.writeFile(`${outputDir}/preview.png`, previewBytes);\\r\\n```\\r\\n- `const workbook = await Workbook.fromCSV(csvText, { sheetName: \\\"Sheet1\\\" })`\\r\\n- `await workbook.fromCSV(csvText, { sheetName: \\\"ImportedData\\\" })` requires an empty workbook; it does not append CSV to a populated workbook. Prefer static `Workbook.fromCSV(...)`.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Worksheet selection/creation\\r\\n- `workbook.worksheets.add(name)`\\r\\n- `workbook.worksheets.getItem(name)`\\r\\n- `workbook.worksheets.getItemAt(index)`\\r\\n- `workbook.worksheets.getActiveWorksheet()` (only after at least one sheet exists)\\r\\n\\r\\n### Worksheet operations\\r\\n- `sheet.getRange(\\\"A1:C10\\\")`, `sheet.getRangeByIndexes(startRow, startCol, rowCount, colCount)`, `sheet.getCell(row, col)`\\r\\n- `sheet.getUsedRange(valuesOnly?)`\\r\\n- `sheet.freezePanes.freezeRows(rowCount)`, `sheet.freezePanes.freezeColumns(columnCount)`, `sheet.freezePanes.unfreeze()`\\r\\n- `sheet.tabColor = \\\"#1F4E78\\\"` sets the worksheet tab color; the getter returns a `Color` object.\\r\\n- `sheet.tables`, `sheet.charts`, `sheet.sparklineGroups` (`sheet.sparklines` alias), `sheet.shapes`, `sheet.images`\\r\\n- `sheet.showGridLines = false`\\r\\n- `sheet.dataTables`, `sheet.conditionalFormattings`, `sheet.dataValidations`\\r\\n- `sheet.deleteAllDrawings()` removes all drawings, including charts, shapes, and images.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Range values/formulas\\r\\n- `const range = sheet.getRange(\\\"A1:C10\\\")`\\r\\n- `range.values = [[...], ...]` (2D matrix of values).\\r\\n- `range.formulas = [[\\\"=...\\\"], ...]`\\r\\n- `range.formulasR1C1 = [[\\\"=RC[-1]*2\\\"]]`\\r\\n- To read: `range.values` / `range.formulas` / `range.displayFormulas` / `range.formulaInfos` (for spill/array formulas)\\r\\n- `range.write(matrixOrPayload)` (auto-sizes/spills from anchor as needed)\\r\\n- `range.writeValues(matrixOrRows)`\\r\\n- `range.fillDown()`, `range.fillRight()`\\r\\n  - `sheet.getRange(\\\"D2\\\").formulas = [[\\\"=...\\\"]]`\\r\\n  - `sheet.getRange(\\\"D2:D200\\\").fillDown()`\\r\\n- `range.clear({ applyTo: \\\"contents\\\" | \\\"formats\\\" | \\\"all\\\" })` clears cell contents, formatting, or both; drawings remain.\\r\\n- `range.copyFrom(sourceRange, \\\"values\\\" | \\\"formulas\\\" | \\\"all\\\")`. Prefer matching shapes; a single-cell value source can broadcast across a larger destination.\\r\\n- `range.copyTo(destRange, \\\"values\\\" | \\\"formulas\\\" | \\\"all\\\")`\\r\\n- `range.offset(rowOffset, colOffset)`, `range.resize(rowCount, columnCount)`, `range.getCurrentRegion()`, `range.getRow(i)`, `range.getColumn(j)`\\r\\n- `range.getRangeByIndexes(startRow, startCol, rowCount, colCount)`, `range.getCell(row, col)` (relative to the range)\\r\\n\\r\\n### Formatting\\r\\n- `range.format` supports `fill`, `font`, `numberFormat`, `borders`, alignments, `wrapText`\\r\\n- `range.format.verticalAlignment = \\\"center\\\"` maps to Excel Middle Align\\r\\n- `range.format.autofitColumns()`, `range.format.autofitRows()`\\r\\n- Excel unit sizing:  `range.format.columnWidth = 18`, `range.format.rowHeight = 24`\\r\\n- Pixel sizing: `range.format.columnWidthPx = 120`, `range.format.rowHeightPx = 24`\\r\\n- `range.setNumberFormat(\\\"yyyy-mm-dd\\\")`\\r\\n- `range.format.numberFormat = [[\\\"0\\\"], [\\\"0.00\\\"], [\\\"@\\\"]]`\\r\\n- Borders: There are two ways to set borders on a range (1) simple form (2) per edge form. Example:\\r\\n```js\\r\\n// (1) Preferred simple form\\r\\n// `preset` can be \\\"none\\\" | \\\"outside\\\" | \\\"inside\\\" | \\\"all\\\" | \\\"doubleBottom\\\"\\r\\n// \\\"outside\\\" will just set borders on the outside edges of the range.\\r\\n// `style` are Excel/OpenXML-style names such as \\\"thin\\\", \\\"medium\\\", \\\"thick\\\", \\\"dashed\\\", and \\\"dotted\\\".\\r\\nrange.format.borders = { preset: \\\"all\\\", style: \\\"thin\\\", color: \\\"#D9D9D9\\\" };\\r\\n\\r\\n// (2) Per-edge form. Use top/bottom/left/right, not edgeTop/edgeBottom/etc.\\r\\n// Format: {style?: string; color?: ColorConfig; weight?: number;}\\r\\n// Prefer setting `style` over `weight` unless you want granular control.\\r\\nrange.format.borders = {\\r\\n  insideHorizontal: { style: \\\"thin\\\", color: \\\"#D9D9D9\\\" },\\r\\n  insideVertical: { style: \\\"medium\\\", color: \\\"#D9D9D9\\\" },\\r\\n  top: { style: \\\"thick\\\", color: \\\"#D9D9D9\\\" },\\r\\n  bottom: { style: \\\"dashed\\\", color: \\\"#D9D9D9\\\" },\\r\\n  left: { style: \\\"dotted\\\", color: \\\"#D9D9D9\\\" },\\r\\n  right: { style: \\\"thin\\\", color: \\\"#D9D9D9\\\" },\\r\\n};\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### Data Validation\\r\\n- `range.dataValidation = { rule: { type: \\\"list\\\", formula1: \\\"Categories!$A$2:$A$4\\\" } }`\\r\\n- `range.dataValidation = { rule: { type: \\\"list\\\", values: [\\\"Not Started\\\", \\\"In Progress\\\"] } }`\\r\\n- `sheet.dataValidations.add({ range: \\\"B2:B100\\\", rule: { type: \\\"whole\\\", operator: \\\"between\\\", formula1: 1, formula2: 10 } })`\\r\\n\\r\\n### Conditional formatting\\r\\n- Use `range.conditionalFormats.add(ruleType, ConditionalFormatConfig);`.\\r\\n- Use `range.conditionalFormats.add(ruleType, {operator, formula, format});`. Choose ruleType, operator, color, and style strings from the inline types below.\\r\\n```\\r\\ntype ConditionalFormatRuleType =\\r\\n  | \\\"cellIs\\\" | \\\"CellValue\\\" | \\\"Custom\\\" | \\\"expression\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"colorScale\\\" | \\\"dataBar\\\" | \\\"iconSet\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"containsText\\\" | \\\"notContainsText\\\" | \\\"beginsWith\\\" | \\\"endsWith\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"containsBlanks\\\" | \\\"notContainsBlanks\\\" | \\\"containsErrors\\\" | \\\"notContainsErrors\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"duplicateValues\\\" | \\\"uniqueValues\\\" | \\\"timePeriod\\\" | \\\"top10\\\" | \\\"aboveAverage\\\";\\r\\n\\r\\ntype CellIsOperator =\\r\\n  | \\\"greaterThan\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"greaterThanOrEqual\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"lessThan\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"lessThanOrEqual\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"equal\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"notEqual\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"between\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"notBetween\\\";\\r\\n\\r\\ntype ConditionalFormatConfig =\\r\\n  | { operator: CellIsOperator; formula: string | number | Array<string | number>; format?: DifferentialFormatConfig }\\r\\n  | { formula: string | number; format?: DifferentialFormatConfig }\\r\\n  | { colors?: ColorConfig[]; thresholds?: CfvoInput[] }\\r\\n  | { color?: ColorConfig; thresholds?: CfvoInput[]; gradient?: boolean }\\r\\n  | { iconSet: string; showValue?: boolean; reverse?: boolean; thresholds?: CfvoInput[] }\\r\\n  | { text: string; format?: DifferentialFormatConfig }\\r\\n  | { timePeriod: \\\"yesterday\\\" | \\\"today\\\" | \\\"tomorrow\\\" | \\\"last7Days\\\" | \\\"lastWeek\\\" | \\\"thisWeek\\\" | \\\"nextWeek\\\" | \\\"lastMonth\\\" | \\\"thisMonth\\\" | \\\"nextMonth\\\"; format?: DifferentialFormatConfig }\\r\\n  | { rank?: number; percent?: boolean; bottom?: boolean; format?: DifferentialFormatConfig }\\r\\n  | { aboveAverage?: boolean; equalAverage?: boolean; stdDev?: number; format?: DifferentialFormatConfig };\\r\\n\\r\\ntype DifferentialFormatConfig = {\\r\\n  fill?: FillConfig;\\r\\n  font?: { bold?: boolean; italic?: boolean; color?: ColorConfig };\\r\\n  border?: RangeBordersConfig; // Per-edge top/bottom/left/right only; presets are ignored here.\\r\\n  numberFormat?: string;\\r\\n};\\r\\n\\r\\ntype CfvoInput =\\r\\n  | \\\"min\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"max\\\"\\r\\n  | number\\r\\n  | `${number}%`\\r\\n  | { type: \\\"min\\\" | \\\"max\\\" | \\\"num\\\" | \\\"percent\\\" | \\\"percentile\\\"; value?: string | number };\\r\\n```\\r\\n- Rule types (`ConditionalFormatRuleType`): \\\"cellIs\\\" | \\\"CellValue\\\" | \\\"Custom\\\" | \\\"expression\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"colorScale\\\" | \\\"dataBar\\\" | \\\"iconSet\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"containsText\\\" | \\\"notContainsText\\\" | \\\"beginsWith\\\" | \\\"endsWith\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"containsBlanks\\\" | \\\"notContainsBlanks\\\" | \\\"containsErrors\\\" | \\\"notContainsErrors\\\"\\r\\n  | \\\"duplicateValues\\\" | \\\"uniqueValues\\\" | \\\"timePeriod\\\" | \\\"top10\\\" | \\\"aboveAverage\\\";\\r\\n- XLSX `iconSet` names: `3Arrows`, `4Arrows`, `5Arrows`, `3ArrowsGray`, `4ArrowsGray`, `5ArrowsGray`, `3TrafficLights1`, `3Signs`, `4RedToBlack`, `3TrafficLights2`, `4TrafficLights`, `3Symbols`, `3Flags`, `3Symbols2`, `5Quarters`, `4Rating`, `5Rating`. Avoid `3Stars`, `3Triangles`, and `5Boxes`: their current XLSX serialization is not reliable.\\r\\n- Custom conditional formatting: `range.conditionalFormats.addCustom(expression, {fill, font, border});`\\r\\n- `range.conditionalFormats.deleteAll()` / `range.conditionalFormats.clear()`\\r\\n\\r\\n```js\\r\\nconst grid = sheet.getRange(\\\"B2:J10\\\");\\r\\ngrid.conditionalFormats.add(\\\"colorScale\\\", {\\r\\n  colors: [\\\"#2563EB\\\", \\\"#FDE047\\\", \\\"#DC2626\\\"],\\r\\n  thresholds: [\\\"min\\\", { type: \\\"percentile\\\", value: 50 }, \\\"max\\\"]\\r\\n});\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### Tables\\r\\n- When adding new tables, set explicit unique names (`TasksTable`, `SummaryTable`).\\r\\n- Do not overlap tables; the API does not reject overlaps. Check existing table ranges in the initial compact `inspect` summary before adding one.\\r\\n- `const table = sheet.tables.add(\\\"A1:H200\\\", true, \\\"TasksTable\\\")`\\r\\n- `table.rows.add(null, [[...], ...])`, `table.getDataRows()`, `table.getHeaderRowRange()`\\r\\n- Read tables: `sheet.tables.items` -> `Table[]`\\r\\n- Set + Getters: `table.name`, `table.style`, `table.showHeaders`\\r\\n- Toggles for table utilities (set/get): `table.showTotals`, `table.showBandedColumns = true`, `table.showFilterButton`\\r\\n- `table.delete()`\\r\\n\\r\\n### Images\\r\\n- `sheet.images.add({dataUrl: \\\"data:image/png;base64,...\\\", anchor: {from: { row: 1, col: 2 }, extent: { widthPx: 160, heightPx: 120 }}})`\\r\\n\\r\\n### Threaded Comments\\r\\nThis creates Excel Comments (threads with replies), which require a user-visible author.\\r\\n- Required: Before adding a comment, create its visible author with `workbook.comments.setSelf({\\\"displayName\\\": <user_display_string>})`, where `<user_display_string>` is a string such as \\\"User\\\"\\r\\n- Create a new thread with a single comment: `const thread = workbook.comments.addThread({\\\"cell\\\": sheet.getRange(\\\"E2\\\")}, \\\"Source: <website>\\\")`\\r\\n- To reply to a threaded comment: `thread.addReply(\\\"This is a reply to the comment\\\")`\\r\\n- To resolve/re-open a thread: `thread.resolve()`, `thread.reopen()`\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Notes\\r\\nExcel Notes are single-cell annotations without replies; threaded Comments are conversations with replies. Both survive XLSX export/import, but PNG renders omit their indicators and text.\\r\\n\\r\\n```js\\r\\nconst authorId = \\\"\\\"; // Or workbook.comments.setSelf({ displayName: \\\"User\\\" }).id\\r\\nworkbook.notes.add({\\r\\n  id: `${sheet.name}:B4`,\\r\\n  target: { cell: { sheetName: sheet.name, sheetId: sheet.sheetId, address: \\\"B4\\\" } },\\r\\n  authorId,\\r\\n  createdAt: \\\"\\\",\\r\\n  body: { plainText: \\\"Source: <website>\\\" },\\r\\n});\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### Charts\\r\\n- Place charts in reserved blank areas with gutter rows/columns; do not cover data.\\r\\n- Prefer range-backed charts so source edits update the chart. Headers are detected when the first row contains text and no numeric cells.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Fast-chart path\\r\\n- For contiguous data: pass categories first, then one column per series:\\r\\n  ```js\\r\\n  sheet.getRange(\\\"F4:H6\\\").values = [\\r\\n    [\\\"Month\\\", \\\"Revenue\\\", \\\"EBITDA\\\"],\\r\\n    [\\\"Jan\\\", 100, 10],\\r\\n    [\\\"Feb\\\", 120, 18],\\r\\n  ];\\r\\n  const chart = sheet.charts.add(\\\"line\\\", sheet.getRange(\\\"F4:H6\\\"));\\r\\n  ```\\r\\n- For nonadjacent columns: pass equal-height, single-column ranges with categories first:\\r\\n  ```js\\r\\n  const chart = sheet.charts.add(\\\"bar\\\", [\\r\\n    sheet.getRange(\\\"A1:A10\\\"),\\r\\n    sheet.getRange(\\\"D1:D10\\\"),\\r\\n    sheet.getRange(\\\"G1:G10\\\"),\\r\\n  ]);\\r\\n  chart.series.items[0].fill = \\\"#F472B6\\\";\\r\\n  ```\\r\\n- Both paths create source-cell references and show a bottom legend. Apply requested formatting once after creation:\\r\\n```js\\r\\nchart.setPosition(\\\"J4\\\", \\\"Q20\\\"); // always set\\r\\nconst fontFamily = \\\"Arial\\\";\\r\\n\\r\\nchart.title = \\\"Revenue and EBITDA Trend\\\";\\r\\n// Set titleTextStyle properties individually, not to an object\\r\\nchart.titleTextStyle.fontSize = 12;\\r\\nchart.titleTextStyle.typeface = fontFamily;\\r\\n\\r\\nchart.legend = { position: \\\"top\\\", textStyle: { typeface: fontFamily } };\\r\\nchart.xAxis = { axisType: \\\"textAxis\\\", textStyle: { typeface: fontFamily, fontSize: 10 } };\\r\\n// Number formatting on chart axis must be set separately even if the source range is already formatted.\\r\\nchart.yAxis = { numberFormatCode: \\\"$#,##0\\\", numberFormatSourceLinked: false, textStyle: { typeface: fontFamily }, tickLabelInterval: 2 };\\r\\nchart.xAxis.title.text = \\\"Month\\\";\\r\\nchart.yAxis.title.text = \\\"Revenue and EBITDA\\\";\\r\\n```\\r\\n- Chart `textStyle.fontSize` uses pixels; `typeface` sets the family. Style axes, legend, and titles separately (`xAxis.textStyle`, `yAxis.textStyle`, `legend.textStyle`, `titleTextStyle`); cell fonts do not configure chart fonts.\\r\\n- Only set/style intended titles; styling an absent title can create a placeholder.\\r\\n- For custom axis formats, set `numberFormatSourceLinked: false`. For $M labels, use `$0.0,,\\\"M\\\"` for dollars, `$0.0,\\\"M\\\"` for thousands, or `$0.0\\\"M\\\"` for millions. Fix formatting without changing source data.\\r\\n- `chart.setData(range)` replaces categories/series but preserves title, legend, and axes. Apply series styling after binding data.\\r\\n- For month/date labels, use helper cells containing strings such as `Jan 2025`; date-axis formats may show serial numbers in previews.\\r\\n- Inspect series through `chart.series.items`; source bindings are `series.formula` and `series.categoryFormula`.\\r\\n- Additional chart getters: `chart.type`, `chart.title.text`, `chart.categories` (may be empty for range-backed charts; inspect series references).\\r\\n- Collection methods: `sheet.charts.getItemOrNullObject(\\\"Chart 1\\\")`, `sheet.charts.deleteAll()`.\\r\\n- XLSX chart types: `\\\"bar\\\" | \\\"line\\\" | \\\"area\\\" | \\\"pie\\\" | \\\"doughnut\\\" | \\\"scatter\\\" | \\\"bubble\\\" | \\\"radar\\\" | \\\"stock\\\"`.\\r\\n- `\\\"treemap\\\"`, `\\\"sunburst\\\"`, `\\\"histogram\\\"`, `\\\"boxWhisker\\\"`, `\\\"waterfall\\\"`, `\\\"funnel\\\"`, and `\\\"map\\\"` are accepted by the API but omitted from XLSX exports in this runtime. Do not use them for Excel deliverables.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Merging cells\\r\\n- `sheet.mergeCells(\\\"A1:C1\\\")`, `sheet.unmergeCells(\\\"A1:C1\\\")`\\r\\n- `range.merge()` merges the target range into one cell; `range.merge(true)` merges across each row in the target range.\\r\\n- `range.unmerge()` reverses a merge.\\r\\nFor example:\\r\\n```js\\r\\nconst range = sheet.getRange(\\\"I23:N24\\\");\\r\\nrange.merge();\\r\\nrange.values = [[\\\"Quarterly revenue\\\"]];\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### Help / Grep\\r\\nUse `workbook.help(...)` primarily for obscure/advanced surfaces (for example deep chart axis settings, unusual drawing configs, pivot APIs, or uncommon option schemas).\\r\\n- `workbook.help(\\\"enum.ShapeGeometry\\\", { include: \\\"index,notes\\\" }).ndjson`\\r\\n- `workbook.help(\\\"enum.*\\\", { search: \\\"ShapeGeometry|LineStyle\\\", include: \\\"index\\\" }).ndjson`\\r\\n- `workbook.help(\\\"shape.add\\\", { include: \\\"examples,notes\\\" }).ndjson`\\r\\n- `workbook.help(\\\"fx.RATE\\\", { include: \\\"index,examples,notes\\\" }).ndjson`\\r\\n- `workbook.help(\\\"cash flow return rate\\\", { search: \\\"IRR|XIRR|NPV|XNPV\\\", include: \\\"index,examples,notes\\\", maxChars: 4000 }).ndjson`\\r\\n- `workbook.help(\\\"*\\\", { search: \\\"fill|borders|autofit\\\", include: \\\"index,examples,notes\\\", maxChars: 6000 }).ndjson`\\r\\n\\r\\n### Trace\\r\\nFor tracing the full tree of how a formula is calculated, `workbook.trace(\\\"Sheet!A1\\\")` is available. For complex formulas, the full tree can be extremely large, so output should be capped or outputted to a separate temp file (never dump raw traces). It takes only a cell reference. \\r\\n\\r\\n## Additional Resources\\r\\n\\r\\n- For sensitivity data tables, read `artifact_tool_docs/DATA_TABLES.md`.\\r\\n- For sparklines, read `artifact_tool_docs/SPARKLINES.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## JavaScript example snippet (runnable)\\r\\n\\r\\n```js\\r\\nimport fs from \\\"node:fs/promises\\\";\\r\\nimport { SpreadsheetFile, Workbook } from fs.mkdir(outputDir, { recursive: true });\\r\\n\\r\\nconst workbook = Workbook.create();\\r\\nconst sheet = workbook.worksheets.add(\\\"Summary\\\");\\r\\n\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"A1:C4\\\").values = [\\r\\n  [\\\"Month\\\", \\\"Revenue\\\", \\\"EBITDA\\\"],\\r\\n  [\\\"Jan\\\", 100, 10],\\r\\n  [\\\"Feb\\\", 120, 18],\\r\\n  [\\\"Mar\\\", 130, 22],\\r\\n];\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"D1\\\").values = [[\\\"Margin\\\"]];\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"D2\\\").formulas = [[\\\"=C2/B2\\\"]];\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"D2:D4\\\").fillDown();\\r\\n\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"A1:G4\\\").format.font = { name: fontFamily, size: 11 };\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"A1:D1\\\").format = {\\r\\n  fill: \\\"#0F766E\\\",\\r\\n  font: { name: fontFamily, bold: true, color: \\\"#FFFFFF\\\" },\\r\\n};\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"B2:C4\\\").format.numberFormat = \\\"$#,##0\\\";\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"D2:D4\\\").format.numberFormat = \\\"0.0%\\\";\\r\\n\\r\\n// Helper range links to source cells so edits update the chart.\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"F1:G1\\\").values = [[\\\"Month\\\", \\\"Revenue\\\"]];\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"F2:G2\\\").formulas = [[\\\"=A2\\\", \\\"=B2\\\"]];\\r\\nsheet.getRange(\\\"F2:G4\\\").fillDown();\\r\\nconst chart = sheet.charts.add(\\\"line\\\", sheet.getRange(\\\"F1:G4\\\"));\\r\\nchart.title = \\\"Revenue Trend\\\";\\r\\nchart.titleTextStyle.typeface = fontFamily;\\r\\nchart.hasLegend = false;\\r\\nchart.xAxis = { axisType: \\\"textAxis\\\", textStyle: { typeface: fontFamily } };\\r\\nchart.yAxis = { numberFormatCode: \\\"$#,##0\\\", numberFormatSourceLinked: false, textStyle: { typeface: fontFamily } };\\r\\nchart.setPosition(\\\"I1\\\", \\\"P15\\\");\\r\\n\\r\\n// Final calculation before visual verification, inspect and export.\\r\\nworkbook.recalculate();\\r\\nconst preview = await workbook.render({\\r\\n  sheetName: \\\"Summary\\\",\\r\\n  autoCrop: \\\"all\\\",\\r\\n  scale: 1,\\r\\n  format: \\\"png\\\",\\r\\n});\\r\\nawait fs.writeFile(`${outputDir}/summary.png`, new Uint8Array(await preview.arrayBuffer()));\\r\\n\\r\\nconst xlsx = await SpreadsheetFile.exportXlsx(workbook);\\r\\nawait xlsx.save(`${outputDir}/summary.xlsx`);\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n\"}}"

Roles do not require separate tabs: keep simple workbooks basic and apply role styling by section on mixed-use tabs.\\r\\n- Keep primary outputs and the assumptions control panel easy to find. Use a dark brand-color tab for outputs and one level lighter for Assumptions; standalone Finance Actuals tabs use light tan. Preserve intentional user/reference colors.\\r\\n- For complex workbooks with several source/internal tabs after the working views, place a divider before that group. Do not add dividers for focused tasks. Choose a plain name that fits the group, such as `Data >>`, `Internal >>` or `Inputs >>`. Color the divider and leave its children uncolored, overriding individual role colors. Give any `ReadMe` tab a distinct muted color, including within a divider group. Leave other tabs uncolored unless color clarifies navigation.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Reader-facing sheet layout\\r\\n\\r\\nFor new reader-facing sheets or authorized restyling, use a compact, presentation-style opening view: a concise unfilled title, modest whitespace and the main results. Choose a table, chart or both to suit the task. Covers follow the simple front-page guidance in Workbook Structure instead of the layout below.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor a filled Cover, start with a bounded area such as `A1:Z100` and adjust it to the intended opening view. Never apply the background to the entire worksheet or whole rows/columns.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Use one blank top row, then a concise unfilled left-aligned title, a thin rule and compact spacing before the main content. Include essential context, not a required subtitle or takeaway. Avoid large filled title banners. This presentation default does not add blank rows to raw source tables or override a supplied layout.\\r\\n- Remove filler. Put useful table commentary in ordinary `Notes` or `Comments` cells to the right, separated by at least one blank spacer column and outside the table header fill and borders. Turn wrapping off. Widen or shorten notes without increasing the table row heights; put longer required explanations in a separate notes area on the same sheet. Keep notes readable and unclipped. Do not add setup/footer blocks. Keep essential units and specific warnings beside the affected result. Follow [Citation Requirements](SKILL.md#citation-requirements) for sources and annotations. These are ordinary cells, not Excel Comments or Notes.\\r\\n- Add summary/KPI cards only when they clarify the requested decision; do not duplicate a small table or make oversized cards.\\r\\n- Choose cards, tables and charts for the task; keep useful trends and comparisons rather than defaulting every summary to a table. Use the width of an analytical Summary: place a compact table beside a useful chart and monthly detail below when that fits the content. Do not turn it into a tall page of prose or shrink fonts to force a layout.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Use a visually clear layout\\r\\n- Distinguish headers, inputs, calculations and notes consistently. Default to dark body text and restrained fills, not a universal teal theme. Preserve domain styling defaults, like finance input/formula/source colors and dynamic statuses.\\r\\n- When cases are used, including outside Finance, show `Case Selected:` and the linked case value (for example, `Base`) prominently near the top of each output and build tab. Center the value horizontally and vertically in a restrained dashed or dotted outline, close to the content. Keep one editable selector and distinguish the active case from actuals and named comparison cases. On Assumptions, put the selector or its linked value and only essential global inputs directly above the driver table. Emphasize each driver's Active Selection row over its case inputs. Include a short case-number key only for numeric selectors. Do not add unused Owner columns or setup sections, and preserve meaningful existing owners.\\r\\n- Put purely technical helper rows, such as period keys, above the model; italicize and group/collapse them where supported. Keep them inspectable but out of business headers and chart labels. Do not hide useful business calculation steps as helpers.\\r\\n- On output tabs, use consistent row heights within each table and modest vertical padding. Tighten excessive spacing and reduce oversized heights after autofit while keeping useful breathing room and readability when tables are copied into slides or printed to PDF. Size rows to the font and content; expand only rows needing wrapped table content, not the whole table, and do not shrink fonts to tighten spacing. Exclude off-table commentary from table-row autofit. Input tabs can use smaller, fitted rows; keep working builds compact. Apply these distinctions by section on mixed-use tabs. Format only populated or intentionally reserved ranges.\\r\\n- Keep working titles, headers and input/calculation areas unmerged. Presentation merges require user/template intent; never overwrite content or combine distinct table columns. In reader-facing tables, center column headers horizontally and vertically. For one heading spanning several otherwise empty header cells, use Center Across Selection where supported, or simplify the layout to one header cell. Treat the span as one header group with no internal borders or blank outlined header boxes; never center across distinct headings or populated cells. Working builds may retain useful blank column headers and genuine spacer columns.\\r\\n- Use yellow/amber for inputs needing updates. Distinguish editable-input cues from calculated warnings, with a compact legend when needed. Follow documented exception styles for overrides, special formulas and one-offs.\\r\\n- Dark column headers: white text with thin white separators between actual headings, including dates.\\r\\n- Section bands: continuous fill and one outside outline, without internal borders; exclude gutters. Keep genuine spacer columns between separate tables or sections blank and unfilled, including their header cells.\\r\\n- If a sheet uses a leading gutter, align titles, sections and tables to the same content edge. Keep it empty except for specified navigation markers. Follow Finance gutter defaults only for financial models; do not impose them on operational trackers.\\r\\n- Prefer thin/light structural borders, stronger section breaks and no full body-cell grid. Do not apply borders around every filled cell. \\r\\n- Hide worksheet gridlines by default on new sheets, including inputs, builds and outputs. Preserve an intentional user/reference setting and do not change gridlines during unrelated edits. Gridline visibility is separate from selective structural borders.\\r\\n- Put needed context in separate cells or ordinary punctuation. Preserve meaningful financial/mathematical labels and symbols, required source quotes, intentional reference conventions and the expressly specified plain `x` navigation markers.\\r\\n- Use bounded conditional formatting for status, risk, variance and exception cues that must react to edits. Reuse the workbook's existing rules rather than inventing business logic for color. Emphasize affected missing/invalid inputs or failed checks, for example with light-red fill and bold red text for critical errors. Keep valid check values neutral. Do not substitute static error paint or decorative PASS fills.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Freeze panes\\r\\n\\r\\n- For tables that need vertical scrolling, freeze the header rows. Freeze identifying columns when horizontal scrolling would hide them. Use the smallest useful frozen area. Leave enough space to read and work with the data.\\r\\n- Do not move content or add tabs to accommodate freezing. Preserve existing panes during unrelated edits.\\r\\n- Do not freeze compact summary, dashboard or cover sheets unless scrolling requires preserving shared headers or row labels.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Align and format by data type\\r\\n\\r\\n- Left-align text, right-align numbers and center column headers horizontally. Default to middle vertical alignment throughout populated and intentionally reserved workbook ranges. Top-align wrapped descriptions where helpful.\\r\\n- Keep numbers/dates typed with explicit, appropriate formats and clear units.
